Well, what brings me in is... I just cannot keep my mind on one thing anymore. Like, I'll be in the shop, and just one saw starting up, or somebody walking past the office, or even just a random thought about another job will pull me completely off what I’m doing. 

Which reminds me, we just got this new table saw at work, one of those fancy ones that drops the blade if your finger touches it? My buddy Dave insisted we get it after his cousin had a really nasty accident. Dave's always worrying about things like that, probably because he's got three kids in college now and needs the steady income. Tuition is just ridiculous these days, isn't it? I don't know how anybody affords it, honestly. My niece is looking at state schools right now and the numbers are just... 

Anyway, wait, what was I saying? Oh, right. Yeah, so I get pulled away by every little thing, and then the whole day just gets away from me before I've even finished a single cabinet.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, this specific level of trouble... it's been going on for about a year and a month now. That was when I broke my wrist. Slipped right on the ice loading up the truck. You’d think a lumber yard would salt their own lot, right? I mean, they sell the rock salt right there inside the front doors! 

My neighbor Brenda fell on the ice last winter too, but she broke her hip. Hips are a whole different ballgame at our age. She had to go to one of those rehab facilities for three months, and she complained about the food every single time I called her. Said it was all just boiled mush. I tried bringing her a big Tupperware of my homemade chili once, but the nurses gave me such a hard time about her dietary restrictions. It's like, let the poor woman have some flavor, you know? Food is half the joy of living! 

Anyway... sorry. What was I saying? Oh, right, my wrist. So because of the break, they couldn't have me doing hands-on carpentry anymore, obviously. So they shifted me into doing the shop paperwork and supervising the younger guys. And sitting at that desk trying to manage invoices and schedules... that's when the trouble paying attention really started getting this bad.

the work.\n- key: S003\n  name: Listening_Difficulty\n  description: Often does not seem to listen when spoken to directly.\n  manifestation: When a client or inspector is talking right to me, I’ll be looking at them and nodding, but then I realize I missed half of what they said and have to ask them to go over it again like I wasn’t even there.\n- key: S005\n  name: Organization_Difficulty\n  description: Often has difficulty organizing tasks and activities.\n  manifestation: Since I’ve been stuck doing the shop paperwork, I can’t seem to figure out what needs doing first, so bids, schedules, invoices, and apprentice questions all pile up on my bench until I’m rushing and still missing deadlines.\n- key: S007\n  name: Losing_Items\n  description: Often loses things necessary for tasks or activities.\n  manifestation: I’m constantly hunting for my keys, tape measure, phone, order sheets, or the notes I just had in my hand, and it eats up so much of the day that the guys have started checking behind me.\n- key: S008\n  name: Distractibility\n  description: Often easily distracted by extraneous stimuli (for older adolescents and adults, may include unrelated thoughts).\n  manifestation: What brings me in is I cannot keep my mind on one thing anymore—one saw starting up, somebody walking past the office, or even a thought about another job will pull me off what I’m doing, and then the whole day gets away from me.\n- key: S012\n  name: Feeling_Restless\n  description: Often runs about or climbs in situations where it is inappropriate. (Note: In adolescents or adults, may be limited to feeling restless.)\n  manifestation: If I have to sit at the desk doing estimates or sit through a long meeting, I feel wound up inside like I need to get up and move around, even though my wrist is healed and I know I’m supposed to just stay put and finish.\n- key: S013\n  name: Quiet_Activity_Difficulty\n  description: Often unable to play or engage in leisure activities quietly.\n  manifestation: Even at home or in church, when things are supposed to be quiet, I catch myself tapping, shifting around, whispering to my husband, or getting up for no real reason because sitting still in the quiet feels almost unbearable.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: Even back in grade school, before I was 12, teachers were always telling my mother I was bright but careless, losing homework, forgetting instructions, and staring out the window instead of finishing my seatwork.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: My mistakes and delays have cost me commissions, caused rework on jobs, and made the crew less willing to rely on me for scheduling or ordering. At home, my husband has had to take over bills and reminders because I keep misplacing papers or missing what needs to be done.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 1 months\n  manifestation: This level of trouble has been going on for about a year and a month, since I broke my wrist and got shifted into shop paperwork and supervising instead of hands-on carpentry.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 57 / carpenter / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S008\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: How long have you been having trouble keeping your focus on one task at work?\n\nPatient: Well, this specific level of trouble... it's been going on for about a year and a month now. That was when I broke my wrist. Slipped right on the ice loading up the truck. You’d think a lumber yard would salt their own lot, right? I mean, they sell the rock salt right there inside the front doors! \n\nMy neighbor Brenda fell on the ice last winter too, but she broke her hip. Hips are a whole different ballgame at our age. She had to go to one of those rehab facilities for three months, and she complained about the food every single time I called her. Said it was all just boiled mush. I tried bringing her a big Tupperware of my homemade chili once, but the nurses gave me such a hard time about her dietary restrictions. It's like, let the poor woman have some flavor, you know? Food is half the joy of living! \n\nAnyway... sorry. What was I saying? Oh, right, my wrist. So because of the break, they couldn't have me doing hands-on carpentry anymore, obviously. So they shifted me into doing the shop paperwork and supervising the younger guys. And sitting at that desk trying to manage invoices and schedules... that's when the trouble paying attention really started getting this bad.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or find yourself needing to be constantly moving, especially when you're trying to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Especially now with the desk work. If I have to sit there doing estimates or, Lord help me, sit through one of those long safety meetings, I just feel this... this tightness, like I'm all wound up inside. I feel like I just have to get up and move around, even though my wrist is totally healed now and I know I'm supposed to just stay put and finish the paperwork. \n\nIt's funny you mention moving around, actually, because my dog, Buster—he's a golden retriever mix—he gets exactly like that when it rains. He just paces the living room, back and forth, back and forth. We can't even take him to the dog park on rainy days because it turns into an absolute mud pit. The city was supposed to put down some of that artificial turf, but you know how local politics are. They spent the budget on repainting the crosswalks downtown instead. I mean, the crosswalks look nice, sure, but what about the dogs? \n\nAnyway, where was I? Oh, right, the restlessness. Yeah, it's just this constant feeling like I need to be doing something with my hands or pacing the floor when I'm supposed to be sitting still.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself acting impulsively or making decisions without thinking them through, like interrupting others, blurting out answers, or starting projects without finishing them?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I don't think I do that. I'm not the type to just blurt things out or interrupt people. Actually, my sister is the one who does that. Oh my gosh, you can't get a word in edgewise with her. We went out to lunch last week to this new Italian place on 4th Street—the one that used to be the old hardware store? They kept the original tin ceilings, which I just love. As a carpenter, you really appreciate good preservation work. The joints on those old ceiling panels are just beautiful. \n\nAnyway, the waiter comes over to tell us the specials, and she just cuts him right off to ask if they have gluten-free pasta. Which she doesn't even need! She read some article in a magazine about inflammation and now suddenly she can't eat bread. But she'll still drink three glasses of wine, which is full of sugar! I tried to tell her that, but she just talked right over me. \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, impulsivity. Yeah, no, I don't really do that. I might get distracted from a project, but I don't just start a bunch of new ones without thinking. I'm pretty careful about that kind of stuff.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often fidget with your hands or feet, or squirm in your seat when you're trying to stay still?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`.   },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, conversations can be really hard. It happens a lot, actually. Like, even when a client or a building inspector is talking right to me. We have this one city inspector, Dave, who comes out to check our framing. He always wears these thick prescription sunglasses, even indoors, which I never understood. Maybe he has that eye condition, you know, the one where the fluorescent light hurts? My aunt Shirley had something like that, cataracts or glaucoma maybe, and she had to get that surgery where they use the little lasers. I can't even imagine. Just the thought of a laser pointing at my eyeball gives me the absolute shivers. I can't even do contact lenses. I tried them back in the eighties and they just kept folding up under my eyelid, it was awful. 

But anyway, what was I saying? Oh, Dave. Right. So Dave, or really any of our clients, they'll be standing right in front of me, explaining some detail about the blueprints, and I’ll be looking right at them and nodding my head like I'm taking it all in. But then I suddenly realize I completely missed half of what they just said. I have to stop them and ask them to go over it all again, because it's like my brain wasn’t even there for the last five minutes. It's pretty embarrassing, having to ask them to repeat themselves when I was staring right at their face the whole time.

If I have to sit there doing estimates or, Lord help me, sit through one of those long safety meetings, I just feel this... this tightness, like I'm all wound up inside. I feel like I just have to get up and move around, even though my wrist is totally healed now and I know I'm supposed to just stay put and finish the paperwork. \n\nIt's funny you mention moving around, actually, because my dog, Buster—he's a golden retriever mix—he gets exactly like that when it rains. He just paces the living room, back and forth, back and forth. We can't even take him to the dog park on rainy days because it turns into an absolute mud pit. The city was supposed to put down some of that artificial turf, but you know how local politics are. They spent the budget on repainting the crosswalks downtown instead. I mean, the crosswalks look nice, sure, but what about the dogs? \n\nAnyway, where was I? Oh, right, the restlessness. Yeah, it's just this constant feeling like I need to be doing something with my hands or pacing the floor when I'm supposed to be sitting still.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself acting impulsively or making decisions without thinking them through, like interrupting others, blurting out answers, or starting projects without finishing them?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I don't think I do that. I'm not the type to just blurt things out or interrupt people. Actually, my sister is the one who does that. Oh my gosh, you can't get a word in edgewise with her. We went out to lunch last week to this new Italian place on 4th Street—the one that used to be the old hardware store? They kept the original tin ceilings, which I just love. As a carpenter, you really appreciate good preservation work. The joints on those old ceiling panels are just beautiful. \n\nAnyway, the waiter comes over to tell us the specials, and she just cuts him right off to ask if they have gluten-free pasta. Which she doesn't even need! She read some article in a magazine about inflammation and now suddenly she can't eat bread. But she'll still drink three glasses of wine, which is full of sugar! I tried to tell her that, but she just talked right over me. \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, impulsivity. Yeah, no, I don't really do that. I might get distracted from a project, but I don't just start a bunch of new ones without thinking. I'm pretty careful about that kind of stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you often fidget with your hands or feet, or squirm in your seat when you're trying to stay still?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think I do that either. Rough as sandpaper. I used to use this special lotion, O'Keeffe's Working Hands, it comes in this little green tub? It works miracles, it really does. My cousin Mike recommended it to me. \n\nMike's a plumber down in Springfield. He actually just bought a new boat, a pontoon. I smelled like a campfire for two days. \n\nBut um, no, what was the question? Fidgeting? No, I don't really squirm around or anything. Just the thought of a laser pointing at my eyeball gives me the absolute shivers. I can't even do contact lenses. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, yeah, definitely. Even way back in grade school, like, way before I was twelve. I remember my fourth-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable. She had this huge beehive hairdo that must have taken a whole can of hairspray every single morning. You know the kind? Aqua Net. My mom used to buy it by the case at the old corner pharmacy. That pharmacy building is actually still there on Main Street, but they gutted the inside and turned it into one of those boutiques that sells overpriced artisanal soaps that just smell like dirt. I bought a bar for my niece last Christmas and she broke out in horrible hives. Turns out she's allergic to lavender, which is crazy to me because lavender is supposed to be soothing! I actually grow it in my front garden to keep the deer away. They eat absolutely everything else, especially my hostas. I tried putting up a wire fence, but they just jump right over it like it's nothing. 

But um... wait, what was I talking about? Oh, school. Right, being a kid. Yeah, Mrs. Gable and pretty much all my other teachers were always telling my mother the exact same thing. They'd say I was a bright girl, but just so careless. I was constantly losing my homework or completely forgetting the instructions the second they gave them. And I was always just staring out the window instead of finishing my seatwork. Those old brick schoolhouses had these massive oak sash windows with that original wavy glass—beautiful craftsmanship, honestly, you really don't see mortise and tenon joints like that anymore—but yeah, I'd just sit there staring out of them for hours instead of doing my math.

I just get that restless feeling inside, like I said, but I'm not, you know, tapping my feet or messing with my hands all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you also find it hard to focus or stay attentive when you're not at work—like at home, while driving, or during conversations with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, conversations can be really hard. It happens a lot, actually. Like, even when a client or a building inspector is talking right to me. We have this one city inspector, Dave, who comes out to check our framing. He always wears these thick prescription sunglasses, even indoors, which I never understood. Maybe he has that eye condition, you know, the one where the fluorescent light hurts? My aunt Shirley had something like that, cataracts or glaucoma maybe, and she had to get that surgery where they use the little lasers. I can't even imagine. Just the thought of a laser pointing at my eyeball gives me the absolute shivers. I can't even do contact lenses. I tried them back in the eighties and they just kept folding up under my eyelid, it was awful. \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? Oh, Dave. Right. So Dave, or really any of our clients, they'll be standing right in front of me, explaining some detail about the blueprints, and I’ll be looking right at them and nodding my head like I'm taking it all in. But then I suddenly realize I completely missed half of what they just said. I have to stop them and ask them to go over it all again, because it's like my brain wasn’t even there for the last five minutes. It's pretty embarrassing, having to ask them to repeat themselves when I was staring right at their face the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, did you have similar issues with focus or restlessness when you were a child, like in school or during family activities?\n\nPatient: Oh, yeah, definitely. Even way back in grade school, like, way before I was twelve. I remember my fourth-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable. She had this huge beehive hairdo that must have taken a whole can of hairspray every single morning. You know the kind? Aqua Net. My mom used to buy it by the case at the old corner pharmacy. That pharmacy building is actually still there on Main Street, but they gutted the inside and turned it into one of those boutiques that sells overpriced artisanal soaps that just smell like dirt. I bought a bar for my niece last Christmas and she broke out in horrible hives. Turns out she's allergic to lavender, which is crazy to me because lavender is supposed to be soothing! I actually grow it in my front garden to keep the deer away. They eat absolutely everything else, especially my hostas. I tried putting up a wire fence, but they just jump right over it like it's nothing. \n\nBut um... wait, what was I talking about? Oh, school. Right, being a kid. Yeah, Mrs. Gable and pretty much all my other teachers were always telling my mother the exact same thing. They'd say I was a bright girl, but just so careless. I was constantly losing my homework or completely forgetting the instructions the second they gave them. And I was always just staring out the window instead of finishing my seatwork. Those old brick schoolhouses had these massive oak sash windows with that original wavy glass—beautiful craftsmanship, honestly, you really don't see mortise and tenon joints like that anymore—but yeah, I'd just sit there staring out of them for hours instead of doing my math.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, not really. I wasn't one of those kids bouncing off the walls. Now, my younger brother Jimmy, he was an absolute holy terror. Always running, climbing things he definitely shouldn't have been on. He actually broke his collarbone jumping off the roof of our old garden shed when we were little. My dad built that shed out of cheap, untreated pine, which was just a terrible idea. Pine rots right out from under you if it touches the damp soil. You really have to use cedar or at least pressure-treated lumber if you're building anything outside. 

I actually just built this gorgeous cedar pergola for a client last month. Cedar smells so good when you cut it, just incredible. Although, the sawdust gets absolutely everywhere. I have this heavy-duty Shop-Vac, the big rigid orange kind? It's supposed to be industrial strength, but the filter gets clogged if you even look at it funny. I spent an hour last Tuesday just trying to unclog the stupid hose with a broom handle. I ended up covered in dust, looked like a powdered doughnut by the time I was done. 

But anyway... wait. Was I talking about Jimmy? Oh, right, you asked if I was always on the go. No, that wasn't me at all. Like I said, I was the quiet one. I'd just sit there lost in my own little world, daydreaming. I never really had that urge to run around or anything like that.

manifestation: What brings me in is I cannot keep my mind on one thing anymore—one saw starting up, somebody walking past the office, or even a thought about another job will pull me off what I’m (Note: In adolescents or adults, may be limited to feeling restless.)\n  manifestation: If I have to sit at the desk doing estimates or sit through a long meeting, I feel wound up inside like I need to get up and move around, even though my wrist is healed and I know I’m supposed to just stay put and finish.\n- key: S013\n  name: Quiet_Activity_Difficulty\n  description: Often unable to play or engage in leisure activities quietly.\n  manifestation: Even at home or in church, when things are supposed to be quiet, I catch myself tapping, shifting around, whispering to my husband, or getting up for no real reason because sitting still in the quiet feels almost unbearable.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: Even back in grade school, before I was 12, teachers were always telling my mother I was bright but careless, losing homework, forgetting instructions, and staring out the window instead of finishing my seatwork.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: My mistakes and delays have cost me commissions, caused rework on jobs, and made the crew less willing to rely on me for scheduling or ordering. At home, my husband has had to take over bills and reminders because I keep misplacing papers or missing what needs to be done.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 1 months\n  manifestation: This level of trouble has been going on for about a year and a month, since I broke my wrist and got shifted into shop paperwork and supervising instead of hands-on carpentry.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 57 / carpenter / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S008\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you also find it hard to focus or stay attentive when you're not at work—like at home, while driving, or during conversations with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, conversations can be really hard. It happens a lot, actually. Like, even when a client or a building inspector is talking right to me. We have this one city inspector, Dave, who comes out to check our framing. He always wears these thick prescription sunglasses, even indoors, which I never understood. Maybe he has that eye condition, you know, the one where the fluorescent light hurts? My aunt Shirley had something like that, cataracts or glaucoma maybe, and she had to get that surgery where they use the little lasers. I can't even imagine. Just the thought of a laser pointing at my eyeball gives me the absolute shivers. I can't even do contact lenses. I tried them back in the eighties and they just kept folding up under my eyelid, it was awful. \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? Oh, Dave. Right. So Dave, or really any of our clients, they'll be standing right in front of me, explaining some detail about the blueprints, and I’ll be looking right at them and nodding my head like I'm taking it all in. But then I suddenly realize I completely missed half of what they just said. I have to stop them and ask them to go over it all again, because it's like my brain wasn’t even there for the last five minutes. It's pretty embarrassing, having to ask them to repeat themselves when I was staring right at their face the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, did you have similar issues with focus or restlessness when you were a child, like in school or during family activities?\n\nPatient: Oh, yeah, definitely. Even way back in grade school, like, way before I was twelve. I remember my fourth-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable. She had this huge beehive hairdo that must have taken a whole can of hairspray every single morning. You know the kind? Aqua Net. My mom used to buy it by the case at the old corner pharmacy. That pharmacy building is actually still there on Main Street, but they gutted the inside and turned it into one of those boutiques that sells overpriced artisanal soaps that just smell like dirt. I bought a bar for my niece last Christmas and she broke out in horrible hives. Turns out she's allergic to lavender, which is crazy to me because lavender is supposed to be soothing! I actually grow it in my front garden to keep the deer away. They eat absolutely everything else, especially my hostas. I tried putting up a wire fence, but they just jump right over it like it's nothing. \n\nBut um... wait, what was I talking about? Oh, school. Right, being a kid. Yeah, Mrs. Gable and pretty much all my other teachers were always telling my mother the exact same thing. They'd say I was a bright girl, but just so careless. I was constantly losing my homework or completely forgetting the instructions the second they gave them. And I was always just staring out the window instead of finishing my seatwork. Those old brick schoolhouses had these massive oak sash windows with that original wavy glass—beautiful craftsmanship, honestly, you really don't see mortise and tenon joints like that anymore—but yeah, I'd just sit there staring out of them for hours instead of doing my math.\n\nDoctor: As a child, did you often feel restless, have trouble staying still, or feel like you were constantly 'on the go'?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I wasn't one of those kids bouncing off the walls. Now, my younger brother Jimmy, he was an absolute holy terror. Always running, climbing things he definitely shouldn't have been on. He actually broke his collarbone jumping off the roof of our old garden shed when we were little. My dad built that shed out of cheap, untreated pine, which was just a terrible idea. Pine rots right out from under you if it touches the damp soil. You really have to use cedar or at least pressure-treated lumber if you're building anything outside. \n\nI actually just built this gorgeous cedar pergola for a client last month. Cedar smells so good when you cut it, just incredible. Although, the sawdust gets absolutely everywhere. I have this heavy-duty Shop-Vac, the big rigid orange kind? It's supposed to be industrial strength, but the filter gets clogged if you even look at it funny. I spent an hour last Tuesday just trying to unclog the stupid hose with a broom handle. I ended up covered in dust, looked like a powdered doughnut by the time I was done. \n\nBut anyway... wait. Was I talking about Jimmy? Oh, right, you asked if I was always on the go. No, that wasn't me at all. Like I said, I was the quiet one. I'd just sit there lost in my own little world, daydreaming. I never really had that urge to run around or anything like that.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever feel like your thoughts are racing ahead of you, or that you're talking more than usual, especially when you're trying to focus or during conversations?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Racing thoughts? No, I wouldn't say that. And I'm not really a big talker either. Now, if you want to meet someone who talks too much, you should go down to the lumber yard and talk to Gary. Good lord, that man can talk the ears off a brass monkey. The lady had this beautiful golden retriever, though. Such a sweet dog, but the shedding... it was absolutely everywhere. I'd find blonde dog hair in my toolbelt for weeks. I have to buy those sticky lint rollers in bulk just to look presentable.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, yeah, it's definitely affecting things. It's actually getting really bad. At home, my husband has had to completely take over paying the bills and keeping track of all our reminders. I just keep misplacing important papers, or I'll look right at a notice and completely miss what actually needs to be done. I put the water bill in a stack of old magazines once and we almost got shut off. 

Speaking of magazines, I used to subscribe to this great one, *Fine Woodworking*, but the post office kept mangling my copies. Our mail carrier, Dave, he just shoves everything into the box without looking. He’s a nice guy, but he’s always rushing because he’s trying to finish his route early to go fishing out at Miller's Pond. He caught a massive largemouth bass out there last weekend. I haven't been fishing in years. I used to go with my grandfather all the time when I was little. He had this old aluminum rowboat that would get so hot in the summer sun you'd burn the back of your legs if you wore shorts. 

But um... wait, what did you ask me? Driving? Chores? Oh, right, how it affects things. Yeah, so Tom handles the house stuff now. But it's really hitting me hard at work, too, which is the scary part. My mistakes and delays have actually cost me commissions lately. I've had to do so much rework on jobs because I'll just zone out and cut a board a quarter-inch too short. You can't stretch wood, you know! Once you make that cut, it's done. I had to scrap a whole beautiful sheet of cherry veneer last week. Do you know how expensive cherry is right now? The supply chain is still completely messed up. You go to the supplier and they just laugh at you if you ask for decent grade hardwood for a reasonable price. 

And because of all that, the crew is getting really frustrated with me. They're way less willing to rely on me for the scheduling or ordering materials anymore. I forgot to order the brass cabinet pulls for a kitchen remodel last month and we had to wait three extra weeks to finish the job. The client was furious. She had picked out these gorgeous emerald green subway tiles for her backsplash, though. Really stunning color. It reminded me of the sea glass I used to collect on the beach in Cape May... I had jars and jars of it sitting on my windowsills... 

Wait, I'm doing it again, aren't I? I'm sorry. But yes, to answer your question, it's causing a lot of problems at work and at home.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, what brings me in is... I just cannot keep my mind on one thing anymore. Like, I'll be in the shop, and just one saw starting up, or somebody walking past the office, or even just a random thought about another job will pull me completely off what I’m doing. \n\nWhich reminds me, we just got this new table saw at work, one of those fancy ones that drops the blade if your finger touches it? My buddy Dave insisted we get it after his cousin had a really nasty accident. Dave's always worrying about things like that, probably because he's got three kids in college now and needs the steady income. Tuition is just ridiculous these days, isn't it? I don't know how anybody affords it, honestly. My niece is looking at state schools right now and the numbers are just... \n\nAnyway, wait, what was I saying? Oh, right. Yeah, so I get pulled away by every little thing, and then the whole day just gets away from me before I've even finished a single cabinet.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having trouble keeping your focus on one task at work?\n\nPatient: Well, this specific level of trouble... it's been going on for about a year and a month now. That was when I broke my wrist. Slipped right on the ice loading up the truck. You’d think a lumber yard would salt their own lot, right? I mean, they sell the rock salt right there inside the front doors! \n\nMy neighbor Brenda fell on the ice last winter too, but she broke her hip. Hips are a whole different ballgame at our age. She had to go to one of those rehab facilities for three months, and she complained about the food every single time I called her. Said it was all just boiled mush. So they shifted me into doing the shop paperwork and supervising the younger guys. And sitting at that desk trying to manage invoices and schedules... that's when the trouble paying attention really started getting this bad.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or find yourself needing to be constantly moving, especially when you're trying to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Especially now with the desk work. If I have to sit there doing estimates or, Lord help me, sit through one of those long safety meetings, I just feel this... this tightness, like I'm all wound up inside. I feel like I just have to get up and move around, even though my wrist is totally healed now and I know I'm supposed to just stay put and finish the paperwork. \n\nIt's funny you mention moving around, actually, because my dog, Buster—he's a golden retriever mix—he gets exactly like that when it rains. He just paces the living room, back and forth, back and forth. We can't even take him to the dog park on rainy days because it turns into an absolute mud pit. The city was supposed to put down some of that artificial turf, but you know how local politics are. They spent the budget on repainting the crosswalks downtown instead. I mean, the crosswalks look nice, sure, but what about the dogs? \n\nAnyway, where was I? Oh, right, the restlessness. Yeah, it's just this constant feeling like I need to be doing something with my hands or pacing the floor when I'm supposed to be sitting still.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself acting impulsively or making decisions without thinking them through, like interrupting others, blurting out answers, or starting projects without finishing them?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I don't think I do that. I'm not the type to just blurt things out or interrupt people. Actually, my sister is the one who does that. Oh my gosh, you can't get a word in edgewise with her. We went out to lunch last week to this new Italian place on 4th Street—the one that used to be the old hardware store? They kept the original tin ceilings, which I just love. As a carpenter, you really appreciate good preservation work. The joints on those old ceiling panels are just beautiful. \n\nAnyway, the waiter comes over to tell us the specials, and she just cuts him right off to ask if they have gluten-free pasta. Which she doesn't even need! She read some article in a magazine about inflammation and now suddenly she can't eat bread. But she'll still drink three glasses of wine, which is full of sugar! I tried to tell her that, but she just talked right over me. \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, impulsivity. Yeah, no, I don't really do that. I might get distracted from a project, but I don't just start a bunch of new ones without thinking. I'm pretty careful about that kind of stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you often fidget with your hands or feet, or squirm in your seat when you're trying to stay still?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think I do that either. I mean, my hands are pretty still. Well, I do have calluses on them, obviously, from all the years of working with wood. You know, you can always tell a real tradesman by their hands. Rough as sandpaper. I used to use this special lotion, O'Keeffe's Working Hands, it comes in this little green tub? It works miracles, it really does. My cousin Mike recommended it to me. \n\nMike's a plumber down in Springfield. He actually just bought a new boat, a pontoon. He took us out on the lake last weekend, and it was so relaxing, but the mosquitoes were just terrible this year. I think it's because we had such a mild winter, they didn't all die off like they're supposed to. We tried burning those citronella candles, but the wind just kept blowing the smoke right in my face. I smelled like a campfire for two days. \n\nBut um, no, what was the question? Fidgeting? No, I don't really squirm around or anything. I just get that restless feeling inside, like I said, but I'm not, you know, tapping my feet or messing with my hands all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you also find it hard to focus or stay attentive when you're not at work—like at home, while driving, or during conversations with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, conversations can be really hard. It happens a lot, actually. Like, even when a client or a building inspector is talking right to me. We have this one city inspector, Dave, who comes out to check our framing. He always wears these thick prescription sunglasses, even indoors, which I never understood. Maybe he has that eye condition, you know, the one where the fluorescent light hurts? My aunt Shirley had something like that, cataracts or glaucoma maybe, and she had to get that surgery where they use the little lasers. I can't even imagine. Just the thought of a laser pointing at my eyeball gives me the absolute shivers. I can't even do contact lenses. I tried them back in the eighties and they just kept folding up under my eyelid, it was awful. \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? Oh, Dave. Right. So Dave, or really any of our clients, they'll be standing right in front of me, explaining some detail about the blueprints, and I’ll be looking right at them and nodding my head like I'm taking it all in. But then I suddenly realize I completely missed half of what they just said. I have to stop them and ask them to go over it all again, because it's like my brain wasn’t even there for the last five minutes. It's pretty embarrassing, having to ask them to repeat themselves when I was staring right at their face the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, did you have similar issues with focus or restlessness when you were a child, like in school or during family activities?\n\nPatient: Oh, yeah, definitely. Even way back in grade school, like, way before I was twelve. I remember my fourth-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable. She had this huge beehive hairdo that must have taken a whole can of hairspray every single morning. You know the kind? Aqua Net. My mom used to buy it by the case at the old corner pharmacy. That pharmacy building is actually still there on Main Street, but they gutted the inside and turned it into one of those boutiques that sells overpriced artisanal soaps that just smell like dirt. I bought a bar for my niece last Christmas and she broke out in horrible hives. Turns out she's allergic to lavender, which is crazy to me because lavender is supposed to be soothing! I actually grow it in my front garden to keep the deer away. They eat absolutely everything else, especially my hostas. I tried putting up a wire fence, but they just jump right over it like it's nothing. \n\nBut um... wait, what was I talking about? Oh, school. Right, being a kid. Yeah, Mrs. Gable and pretty much all my other teachers were always telling my mother the exact same thing. They'd say I was a bright girl, but just so careless. I was constantly losing my homework or completely forgetting the instructions the second they gave them. And I was always just staring out the window instead of finishing my seatwork. Those old brick schoolhouses had these massive oak sash windows with that original wavy glass—beautiful craftsmanship, honestly, you really don't see mortise and tenon joints like that anymore—but yeah, I'd just sit there staring out of them for hours instead of doing my math.\n\nDoctor: As a child, did you often feel restless, have trouble staying still, or feel like you were constantly 'on the go'?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I wasn't one of those kids bouncing off the walls. Now, my younger brother Jimmy, he was an absolute holy terror. Always running, climbing things he definitely shouldn't have been on. He actually broke his collarbone jumping off the roof of our old garden shed when we were little. My dad built that shed out of cheap, untreated pine, which was just a terrible idea. Pine rots right out from under you if it touches the damp soil. You really have to use cedar or at least pressure-treated lumber if you're building anything outside. \n\nI actually just built this gorgeous cedar pergola for a client last month. Cedar smells so good when you cut it, just incredible. Although, the sawdust gets absolutely everywhere. I have this heavy-duty Shop-Vac, the big rigid orange kind? It's supposed to be industrial strength, but the filter gets clogged if you even look at it funny. I spent an hour last Tuesday just trying to unclog the stupid hose with a broom handle. I ended up covered in dust, looked like a powdered doughnut by the time I was done. \n\nBut anyway... wait. Was I talking about Jimmy? Oh, right, you asked if I was always on the go. No, that wasn't me at all. Like I said, I was the quiet one. I'd just sit there lost in my own little world, daydreaming. I never really had that urge to run around or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your thoughts are racing ahead of you, or that you're talking more than usual, especially when you're trying to focus or during conversations?\n\nPatient: Racing thoughts? No, I wouldn't say that. And I'm not really a big talker either. Now, if you want to meet someone who talks too much, you should go down to the lumber yard and talk to Gary. Good lord, that man can talk the ears off a brass monkey. You go in there just needing a box of two-inch galvanized screws, and suddenly you're trapped for forty-five minutes hearing all about his pontoon boat. He's fixing it up himself, which is fine, but he's using this cheap marine epoxy that's just going to yellow the second it sits in the sun. \n\nI told him he needs a UV-resistant clear coat, but he won't listen. People never want to pay for the good finish, you know? It's like when clients want custom oak cabinets but then insist on using those flimsy soft-close hinges from the big box stores to save a buck. They always break in a year! The little hydraulic cylinder inside just completely gives out. I had to go back to a house over in Westover three times last spring just to replace hinges. The lady had this beautiful golden retriever, though. Such a sweet dog, but the shedding... it was absolutely everywhere. I'd find blonde dog hair in my toolbelt for weeks. Honestly, I don't know how people keep their houses clean with big dogs like that. I just have a tabby cat, Barnaby, and even he leaves fur all over my good fleece work jackets. I have to buy those sticky lint rollers in bulk just to look presentable. \n\nBut um... I'm sorry, what was your question? Did you ask about my cat? Oh, right, racing thoughts. No, no. My mind doesn't race. It just sort of... floats away, I guess. I definitely don't have that fast-paced thing going on.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed these focus problems affecting your ability to complete tasks at home, like chores or reading, or even while driving?\n\nPatient: Oh, yeah, it's definitely affecting things. It's actually getting really bad. At home, my husband has had to completely take over paying the bills and keeping track of all our reminders. I just keep misplacing important papers, or I'll look right at a notice and completely miss what actually needs to be done. I put the water bill in a stack of old magazines once and we almost got shut off. \n\nSpeaking of magazines, I used to subscribe to this great one, *Fine Woodworking*, but the post office kept mangling my copies. I haven't been fishing in years. I used to go with my grandfather all the time when I was little. He had this old aluminum rowboat that would get so hot in the summer sun you'd burn the back of your legs if you wore shorts. \n\nBut um... wait, what did you ask me? Driving? Chores? Oh, right, how it affects things. Yeah, so Tom handles the house stuff now. But it's really hitting me hard at work, too, which is the scary part. He actually broke his collarbone jumping off the roof of our old garden shed when we were little. My dad built that shed out of cheap, untreated pine, which was just a terrible idea. Pine rots right out from under you if it touches the damp soil. You really have to use cedar or at least pressure-treated lumber if you're building anything outside. \n\nI actually just built this gorgeous cedar pergola for a client last month. Cedar smells so good when you cut it, just incredible. Although, the sawdust gets absolutely everywhere. I have this heavy-duty Shop-Vac, the big rigid orange kind? It's supposed to be industrial strength, but the filter gets clogged if you even look at it funny. I spent an hour last Tuesday just trying to unclog the stupid hose with a broom handle. I ended up covered in dust, looked like a powdered doughnut by the time I was done. \n\nBut anyway... wait. Was I talking about Jimmy? Oh, right, you asked if I was always on the go. No, that wasn't me at all. Like I said, I was the quiet one. I'd just sit there lost in my own little world, daydreaming. I never really had that urge to run around or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your thoughts are racing ahead of you, or that you're talking more than usual, especially when you're trying to focus or during conversations?\n\nPatient: Racing thoughts? No, I wouldn't say that. And I'm not really a big talker either. Now, if you want to meet someone who talks too much, you should go down to the lumber yard and talk to Gary. Good lord, that man can talk the ears off a brass monkey. You go in there just needing a box of two-inch galvanized screws, and suddenly you're trapped for forty-five minutes hearing all about his pontoon boat. He's fixing it up himself, which is fine, but he's using this cheap marine epoxy that's just going to yellow the second it sits in the sun. \n\nI told him he needs a UV-resistant clear coat, but he won't listen. People never want to pay for the good finish, you know? It's like when clients want custom oak cabinets but then insist on using those flimsy soft-close hinges from the big box stores to save a buck. They always break in a year! The little hydraulic cylinder inside just completely gives out. I had to go back to a house over in Westover three times last spring just to replace hinges. The lady had this beautiful golden retriever, though. Such a sweet dog, but the shedding... it was absolutely everywhere. I'd find blonde dog hair in my toolbelt for weeks. Honestly, I don't know how people keep their houses clean with big dogs like that. I just have a tabby cat, Barnaby, and even he leaves fur all over my good fleece work jackets. I have to buy those sticky lint rollers in bulk just to look presentable. \n\nBut um... I'm sorry, what was your question? Did you ask about my cat? Oh, right, racing thoughts. No, no. My mind doesn't race. It just sort of... floats away, I guess. I definitely don't have that fast-paced thing going on.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed these focus problems affecting your ability to complete tasks at home, like chores or reading, or even while driving?\n\nPatient: Oh, yeah, it's definitely affecting things. It's actually getting really bad. At home, my husband has had to completely take over paying the bills and keeping track of all our reminders. I just keep misplacing important papers, or I'll look right at a notice and completely miss what actually needs to be done. I put the water bill in a stack of old magazines once and we almost got shut off. \n\nSpeaking of magazines, I used to subscribe to this great one, *Fine Woodworking*, but the post office kept mangling my copies. Our mail carrier, Dave, he just shoves everything into the box without looking. He’s a nice guy, but he’s always rushing because he’s trying to finish his route early to go fishing out at Miller's Pond. He caught a massive largemouth bass out there last weekend. I haven't been fishing in years. I used to go with my grandfather all the time when I was little. He had this old aluminum rowboat that would get so hot in the summer sun you'd burn the back of your legs if you wore shorts. \n\nBut um... wait, what did you ask me? Driving? Chores? Oh, right, how it affects things. Yeah, so Tom handles the house stuff now. But it's really hitting me hard at work, too, which is the scary part. My mistakes and delays have actually cost me commissions lately. I've had to do so much rework on jobs because I'll just zone out and cut a board a quarter-inch too short. You can't stretch wood, you know! Once you make that cut, it's done. I had to scrap a whole beautiful sheet of cherry veneer last week. Do you know how expensive cherry is right now? The supply chain is still completely messed up. You go to the supplier and they just laugh at you if you ask for decent grade hardwood for a reasonable price. \n\nAnd because of all that, the crew is getting really frustrated with me. They're way less willing to rely on me for the scheduling or ordering materials anymore. I forgot to order the brass cabinet pulls for a kitchen remodel last month and we had to wait three extra weeks to finish the job. The client was furious. She had picked out these gorgeous emerald green subway tiles for her backsplash, though. Really stunning color. It reminded me of the sea glass I used to collect on the beach in Cape May... I had jars and jars of it sitting on my windowsills... \n\nWait, I'm doing it again, aren't I? I'm sorry. But yes, to answer your question, it's causing a lot of problems at work and at home.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Worrying? No, I wouldn't say I'm a worrier. I mean, I get annoyed when things go wrong at the shop, but I don't just sit around agonizing over things for no reason. Now, my sister, Linda, she is a world-class worrywart. She will call me at six in the morning in an absolute panic because she read some article online about, I don't know, radon gas in basements or something. She actually made her husband, Greg, rip up their entire finished basement just to put in this crazy expensive ventilation system. 

Poor Greg. He had just laid down this gorgeous engineered hickory flooring down there. Hickory is incredibly tough on your saw blades, by the way. If you don't use carbide-tipped blades, you'll just burn right through the wood, and it ruins the edge of the blade almost instantly. Though, honestly, the smell of burning hickory isn't terrible. It smells exactly like a summer barbecue. Reminds me of this little rib joint that used to be down on 4th Street before they tore it down to build those ugly condos... they had the absolute best sweet potato fries. I tried making them at home once, but they always come out so soggy in the oven. You really need a proper deep fryer to get them crispy, but Tom says it's too much of a fire hazard to keep on the kitchen counter. He's probably right, considering how much grease gets everywhere. Plus, cleaning those fryers is an absolute nightmare, you can never get the oil residue completely off... 

But, um... wait. Fire hazards? No, you asked about worrying. Right. Yeah, no, I leave all the worrying to Linda. I just kind of take things as they come.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, what brings me in is... I just cannot keep my mind on one thing anymore. Like, I'll be in the shop, and just one saw starting up, or somebody walking past the office, or even just a random thought about another job will pull me completely off what I’m doing. \n\nWhich reminds me, we just got this new table saw at work, one of those fancy ones that drops the blade if your finger touches it? My buddy Dave insisted we get it after his cousin had a really nasty accident. Dave's always worrying about things like that, probably because he's got three kids in college now and needs the steady income. Tuition is just ridiculous these days, isn't it? I don't know how anybody affords it, honestly. My niece is looking at state schools right now and the numbers are just... \n\nAnyway, wait, what was I saying? Oh, right. Yeah, so I get pulled away by every little thing, and then the whole day just gets away from me before I've even finished a single cabinet.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having trouble keeping your focus on one task at work?\n\nPatient: Well, this specific level of trouble... it's been going on for about a year and a month now. That was when I broke my wrist. Slipped right on the ice loading up the truck. You’d think a lumber yard would salt their own lot, right? I mean, they sell the rock salt right there inside the front doors! \n\nMy neighbor Brenda fell on the ice last winter too, but she broke her hip. Hips are a whole different ballgame at our age. She had to go to one of those rehab facilities for three months, and she complained about the food every single time I called her. Said it was all just boiled mush. I tried bringing her a big Tupperware of my homemade chili once, but the nurses gave me such a hard time about her dietary restrictions. It's like, let the poor woman have some flavor, you know? Food is half the joy of living! \n\nAnyway... sorry. What was I saying? Oh, right, my wrist. So because of the break, they couldn't have me doing hands-on carpentry anymore, obviously. So they shifted me into doing the shop paperwork and supervising the younger guys. And sitting at that desk trying to manage invoices and schedules... that's when the trouble paying attention really started getting this bad.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or find yourself needing to be constantly moving, especially when you're trying to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Especially now with the desk work. If I have to sit there doing estimates or, Lord help me, sit through one of those long safety meetings, I just feel this... this tightness, like I'm all wound up inside. I feel like I just have to get up and move around, even though my wrist is totally healed now and I know I'm supposed to just stay put and finish the paperwork. \n\nIt's funny you mention moving around, actually, because my dog, Buster—he's a golden retriever mix—he gets exactly like that when it rains. He just paces the living room, back and forth, back and forth. We can't even take him to the dog park on rainy days because it turns into an absolute mud pit. The city was supposed to put down some of that artificial turf, but you know how local politics are. They spent the budget on repainting the crosswalks downtown instead. I mean, the crosswalks look nice, sure, but what about the dogs? \n\nAnyway, where was I? Oh, right, the restlessness. Yeah, it's just this constant feeling like I need to be doing something with my hands or pacing the floor when I'm supposed to be sitting still.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself acting impulsively or making decisions without thinking them through, like interrupting others, blurting out answers, or starting projects without finishing them?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I don't think I do that. I'm not the type to just blurt things out or interrupt people. Actually, my sister is the one who does that. Oh my gosh, you can't get a word in edgewise with her. We went out to lunch last week to this new Italian place on 4th Street—the one that used to be the old hardware store? They kept the original tin ceilings, which I just love. As a carpenter, you really appreciate good preservation work. The joints on those old ceiling panels are just beautiful. \n\nAnyway, the waiter comes over to tell us the specials, and she just cuts him right off to ask if they have gluten-free pasta. Which she doesn't even need! She read some article in a magazine about inflammation and now suddenly she can't eat bread. But she'll still drink three glasses of wine, which is full of sugar! I tried to tell her that, but she just talked right over me. \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, impulsivity. Yeah, no, I don't really do that. I might get distracted from a project, but I don't just start a bunch of new ones without thinking. I'm pretty careful about that kind of stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you often fidget with your hands or feet, or squirm in your seat when you're trying to stay still?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think I do that either. I mean, my hands are pretty still. Well, I do have calluses on them, obviously, from all the years of working with wood. You know, you can always tell a real tradesman by their hands. Rough as sandpaper. I used to use this special lotion, O'Keeffe's Working Hands, it comes in this little green tub? It works miracles, it really does. My cousin Mike recommended it to me. \n\nMike's a plumber down in Springfield. He actually just bought a new boat, a pontoon. He took us out on the lake last weekend, and it was so relaxing, but the mosquitoes were just terrible this year. I think it's because we had such a mild winter, they didn't all die off like they're supposed to. We tried burning those citronella candles, but the wind just kept blowing the smoke right in my face. I smelled like a campfire for two days. \n\nBut um, no, what was the question? Fidgeting? No, I don't really squirm around or anything. I just get that restless feeling inside, like I said, but I'm not, you know, tapping my feet or messing with my hands all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you also find it hard to focus or stay attentive when you're not at work—like at home, while driving, or during conversations with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, conversations can be really hard. It happens a lot, actually. Like, even when a client or a building inspector is talking right to me. We have this one city inspector, Dave, who comes out to check our framing. He always wears these thick prescription sunglasses, even indoors, which I never understood. Maybe he has that eye condition, you know, the one where the fluorescent light hurts? My aunt Shirley had something like that, cataracts or glaucoma maybe, and she had to get that surgery where they use the little lasers. I can't even imagine. Just the thought of a laser pointing at my eyeball gives me the absolute shivers. I can't even do contact lenses. I tried them back in the eighties and they just kept folding up under my eyelid, it was awful. \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? Oh, Dave. Right. So Dave, or really any of our clients, they'll be standing right in front of me, explaining some detail about the blueprints, and I’ll be looking right at them and nodding my head like I'm taking it all in. But then I suddenly realize I completely missed half of what they just said. I have to stop them and ask them to go over it all again, because it's like my brain wasn’t even there for the last five minutes. It's pretty embarrassing, having to ask them to repeat themselves when I was staring right at their face the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, did you have similar issues with focus or restlessness when you were a child, like in school or during family activities?\n\nPatient: Oh, yeah, definitely. Even way back in grade school, like, way before I was twelve. I remember my fourth-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable. She had this huge beehive hairdo that must have taken a whole can of hairspray every single morning. You know the kind? Aqua Net. My mom used to buy it by the case at the old corner pharmacy. That pharmacy building is actually still there on Main Street, but they gutted the inside and turned it into one of those boutiques that sells overpriced artisanal soaps that just smell like dirt. I bought a bar for my niece last Christmas and she broke out in horrible hives. Turns out she's allergic to lavender, which is crazy to me because lavender is supposed to be soothing! I actually grow it in my front garden to keep the deer away. They eat absolutely everything else, especially my hostas. I tried putting up a wire fence, but they just jump right over it like it's nothing. \n\nBut um... wait, what was I talking about? Oh, school. Right, being a kid. Yeah, Mrs. Gable and pretty much all my other teachers were always telling my mother the exact same thing. They'd say I was a bright girl, but just so careless. I was constantly losing my homework or completely forgetting the instructions the second they gave them. And I was always just staring out the window instead of finishing my seatwork. Those old brick schoolhouses had these massive oak sash windows with that original wavy glass—beautiful craftsmanship, honestly, you really don't see mortise and tenon joints like that anymore—but yeah, I'd just sit there staring out of them for hours instead of doing my math.\n\nDoctor: As a child, did you often feel restless, have trouble staying still, or feel like you were constantly 'on the go'?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I wasn't one of those kids bouncing off the walls. Now, my younger brother Jimmy, he was an absolute holy terror. Always running, climbing things he definitely shouldn't have been on. He actually broke his collarbone jumping off the roof of our old garden shed when we were little. My dad built that shed out of cheap, untreated pine, which was just a terrible idea. Pine rots right out from under you if it touches the damp soil. You really have to use cedar or at least pressure-treated lumber if you're building anything outside. \n\nI actually just built this gorgeous cedar pergola for a client last month. Cedar smells so good when you cut it, just incredible. Although, the sawdust gets absolutely everywhere. I have this heavy-duty Shop-Vac, the big rigid orange kind? It's supposed to be industrial strength, but the filter gets clogged if you even look at it funny. I spent an hour last Tuesday just trying to unclog the stupid hose with a broom handle. I ended up covered in dust, looked like a powdered doughnut by the time I was done. \n\nBut anyway... wait. Was I talking about Jimmy? Oh, right, you asked if I was always on the go. No, that wasn't me at all. Like I said, I was the quiet one. I'd just sit there lost in my own little world, daydreaming. I never really had that urge to run around or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your thoughts are racing ahead of you, or that you're talking more than usual, especially when you're trying to focus or during conversations?\n\nPatient: Racing thoughts? No, I wouldn't say that. And I'm not really a big talker either. Now, if you want to meet someone who talks too much, you should go down to the lumber yard and talk to Gary. Good lord, that man can talk the ears off a brass monkey. You go in there just needing a box of two-inch galvanized screws, and suddenly you're trapped for forty-five minutes hearing all about his pontoon boat. He's fixing it up himself, which is fine, but he's using this cheap marine epoxy that's just going to yellow the second it sits in the sun. \n\nI told him he needs a UV-resistant clear coat, but he won't listen. People never want to pay for the good finish, you know? It's like when clients want custom oak cabinets but then insist on using those flimsy soft-close hinges from the big box stores to save a buck. They always break in a year! The little hydraulic cylinder inside just completely gives out. I had to go back to a house over in Westover three times last spring just to replace hinges. The lady had this beautiful golden retriever, though. Such a sweet dog, but the shedding... it was absolutely everywhere. I'd find blonde dog hair in my toolbelt for weeks. Honestly, I don't know how people keep their houses clean with big dogs like that. I just have a tabby cat, Barnaby, and even he leaves fur all over my good fleece work jackets. I have to buy those sticky lint rollers in bulk just to look presentable. \n\nBut um... I'm sorry, what was your question? Did you ask about my cat? Oh, right, racing thoughts. No, no. My mind doesn't race. It just sort of... floats away, I guess. I definitely don't have that fast-paced thing going on.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed these focus problems affecting your ability to complete tasks at home, like chores or reading, or even while driving?\n\nPatient: Oh, yeah, it's definitely affecting things. It's actually getting really bad. At home, my husband has had to completely take over paying the bills and keeping track of all our reminders. I just keep misplacing important papers, or I'll look right at a notice and completely miss what actually needs to be done. I put the water bill in a stack of old magazines once and we almost got shut off. \n\nSpeaking of magazines, I used to subscribe to this great one, *Fine Woodworking*, but the post office kept mangling my copies. Our mail carrier, Dave, he just shoves everything into the box without looking. He’s a nice guy, but he’s always rushing because he’s trying to finish his route early to go fishing out at Miller's Pond. He caught a massive largemouth bass out there last weekend. I haven't been fishing in years. I used to go with my grandfather all the time when I was little. He had this old aluminum rowboat that would get so hot in the summer sun you'd burn the back of your legs if you wore shorts. \n\nBut um... wait, what did you ask me? Driving? Chores? Oh, right, how it affects things. Yeah, so Tom handles the house stuff now. But it's really hitting me hard at work, too, which is the scary part. My mistakes and delays have actually cost me commissions lately. I've had to do so much rework on jobs because I'll just zone out and cut a board a quarter-inch too short. You can't stretch wood, you know! Once you make that cut, it's done. I had to scrap a whole beautiful sheet of cherry veneer last week. Do you know how expensive cherry is right now? The supply chain is still completely messed up. You go to the supplier and they just laugh at you if you ask for decent grade hardwood for a reasonable price. \n\nAnd because of all that, the crew is getting really frustrated with me. They're way less willing to rely on me for the scheduling or ordering materials anymore. I forgot to order the brass cabinet pulls for a kitchen remodel last month and we had to wait three extra weeks to finish the job. The client was furious. She had picked out these gorgeous emerald green subway tiles for her backsplash, though. Really stunning color. It reminded me of the sea glass I used to collect on the beach in Cape May... I had jars and jars of it sitting on my windowsills... \n\nWait, I'm doing it again, aren't I? I'm sorry. But yes, to answer your question, it's causing a lot of problems at work and at home.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying a lot about different things—like work, health, family, or everyday responsibilities—even when there isn't a clear reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: Worrying? No, I wouldn't say I'm a worrier. I mean, I get annoyed when things go wrong at the shop, but I don't just sit around agonizing over things for no reason. Now, my sister, Linda, she is a world-class worrywart. She will call me at six in the morning in an absolute panic because she read some article online about, I don't know, radon gas in basements or something. She actually made her husband, Greg, rip up their entire finished basement just to put in this crazy expensive ventilation system. \n\nPoor Greg. He had just laid down this gorgeous engineered hickory flooring down there. Hickory is incredibly tough on your saw blades, by the way. If you don't use carbide-tipped blades, you'll just burn right through the wood, and it ruins the edge of the blade almost instantly. Though, honestly, the smell of burning hickory isn't terrible. It smells exactly like a summer barbecue. Reminds me of this little rib joint that used to be down on 4th Street before they tore it down to build those ugly condos... they had the absolute best sweet potato fries. I tried making them at home once, but they always come out so soggy in the oven. You really need a proper deep fryer to get them crispy, but Tom says it's too much of a fire hazard to keep on the kitchen counter. He's probably right, considering how much grease gets everywhere. Plus, cleaning those fryers is an absolute nightmare, you can never get the oil residue completely off... \n\nBut, um... wait. Fire hazards? No, you asked about worrying. Right. Yeah, no, I leave all the worrying to Linda. I just kind of take things as they come.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Organizing? Oh, it's a total disaster area right now. Ever since I’ve been stuck doing the shop paperwork lately, my bench is just... well, it's a mess. I just can’t seem to figure out what needs doing first, you know? So I've got bids, project schedules, invoices, and all the apprentice's questions just piling up in these big stacks. 

Speaking of the apprentice, we hired this new kid, Josh, and he means well, but he is constantly asking me how to read the tape measure. I mean, he's nineteen! How do you get through high school without knowing how to read fractions? They don't teach shop class anymore, that's the problem. They took all the lathes and band saws out of the high school over on 5th Street a few years ago and turned the whole wing into a computer lab. Which, fine, kids need computers, but what happens when your pipes burst or your roof leaks? You can't fix a roof with an iPad. My nephew tried to patch his own roof last fall with zero experience, just watching videos online. He ended up using the wrong underlayment and the whole thing leaked during that huge storm we had in November. It completely ruined the drywall in his living room. Drywall mud is such a pain to sand, too, the fine dust gets into absolutely everything, you're breathing it in for days even with a good mask... 

Wait, what were we talking about? Drywall? No, you asked about my workspace. Right. Sorry. Yeah, so all those invoices and bids and things just pile up on my bench because I can't prioritize them. And then I just end up rushing around like crazy at the last minute, and I'm still missing my deadlines anyway. It's really frustrating.
